But I can also change my posterior beliefs about the parameters of my generative model.

So we have a nice distinction now between sort of inference and learning.

And I'm using active learning in the sort of traditional sense of certainly promoted by people like David McKay in the 1980s and 90s.

And the difference here is exactly the same maths. It's just it usually unfolds over progressively longer timescales.

So I have to do lots of evidence accumulation or information gathering to resolve uncertainty about states of the world and how they change.

And then on the basis of those beliefs, I accumulate those beliefs and assimilate that into optimising my beliefs about the parameters,

say the connection weights in a neural network or the Dirichlet counts in a tensor network that would correspond to the slow process of free energy minimisation.

That basically is minimising the complexity of my model of the world maximising its compressibility, maximising the mutual information,

all of these being equivalent perspectives on the same imperative to minimise, to maximise the marginal likelihood or the model evidence.

The final thing though, is that not only can we change our posterior beliefs encoded by the, you know, our internal states,

we can also minimise the free energy by changing our prior beliefs.

My name is William Gebhardt.

I am representing the Neuroadaptive Computing Laboratory from the Rochester Institute of Technology in New York.

I'm here today to talk to you about NGC Learn, specifically the V3, which is our upcoming release of NGC Learn.

It is a fast and modular computational neuroscience library.

And with the acquisition of V3, it just gives you a bunch of new tools to work with.

So first off, I just want to give a very brief overview on what computational neuroscience actually is.

So computational neuroscience at a glance is basically just the study and investigation of the brain functions using mathematical computational tools and theories.

And what this means is that instead of going to math first and then trying to claim that we are, you know, using things like the brain, we go to the brain first and then try and model the processes that happen in the brain with math.

Your brain is not actively doing a bunch of matrix multiplication.

Look at memory.

And these are all just the various things that we look at in computational neuroscience.

Now, what this kind of means is that we need tools to work on this because the brain is complex.

For example, if when our brain is interpreting the world, it is receiving lots of data, like gigabytes and gigabytes and gigabytes of data coming in constantly.

And what this means is that if we have a model, we need to obviously we're just going to be looking at a small pared down version of the amount of data that comes into our brain.

So we're always working with a slightly suboptimal picture.

But on top of that, your brain isn't ever idle.

So in traditional deep neural, like deep learning, you take an image, you feed it into your model and then you get an output.

So sometimes we do things what's known as recurrent neural networks where you give it an input, it goes and it processes and then you give it another input and it uses the output of the previous one and the new input and it processes.

That's closer to what your brain does.

But the problem there is all of the little internal parts of that network don't have values.

And so like we want to make sure that we're representing things with biology.

Sorry, was there somebody speaking?

Okay, sorry.

And so then this biological interpretation also lends itself to how we get inputs into our models.

So for instance, the focal point of your human eye can only really process things at roughly 60 frames a second.

Your periphery processes things a lot faster than that.

But the focal point doesn't process much faster than that.

But in between those frames, it doesn't sit idle.

And then obviously different sensors in our bodies sense things at different times.

For instance, the periphery, the rods in our eyes process things a lot faster than the cones in our eyes.

And so then we work inside of this biological framing that I was alluding to earlier, where when we model our neurons,

when we model our neurons, we want to make sure that they are stateful and that we are working in a more time aware system.

And we do this because your brain is a time aware system.

And so we still use a lot of the similar words that you're going to hear in traditional deep learning.

But for us, when we say we are talking about a synapse, we are talking about the actual modeled connection of the synapse.

When we're talking about a neuron, we are talking about voltages, currents, membrane potentials.

We are talking about the physical, biological aspects of a neuron.

And what this also means in our biological framing is that the way that information is transmitted from point A in our network to point B in our network,

it can't just send a floating point number because your brain doesn't send floating point numbers.

The way neurons communicate in our brains is with pulses.

For us, we call them spikes.

And so this in and of itself makes things a lot more complicated when it comes to modeling.

Because if we're sending discrete pulses over time, we have to do a full forward pass over our model to figure out where pulses happen,

send pulses, and then we have to do that again and again and again and again.

And basically what it looks like at the end of all of this is we have about a 200-layer recurrent network where it's just going down for like 200 time steps.

But really, it's not a recurrent network.

It's just constantly processing new information and constantly giving you some output, but that output might just be null.

So all of this comes together to basically say that everything works better as just one giant state machine.

So we have our model.

We're calling it S.

S.

There's something going.

Like there's always values that exist.

And the main thing to keep in mind with all of this is that these different initial conditions and initial starting state vastly change the model's output, even if it's shown the same information.

So that's kind of the overview of computational neuroscience, state for modeling, and kind of setting up our problem space.

Now, this is not a field that came about two years ago.

This is a field that people have been working and tinkering around in for a little bit of time now.

So that might break the question of, well, somebody probably has built something before, and they have.

I'm going.

I'm going.

But the thing about building previously built models is that they kind of generally operate as black boxes.

So because building these large stateful systems incurs a lot of overhead, most libraries that exist today hide a lot of what's going on.

And you have some very specific tools that you can use to modify the internals of your system.

But you don't necessarily have all the tools that you want.

If you want to come up with a new learning rule, maybe you don't have all of the values that you need for that learning rule.

And there's no good way of modifying the system internally.

And so testing new components and everything is not easy.

It's not trivial.

It's something that takes a fair amount of time.

And if we're testing models that take a long time to run, it means that you can just lose days, weeks to just trying to get a model to see if it'll do anything.

And you don't know whether or not it'll work.

And just as a reference point here, some of these alternative models take up to like eight hours to do an EPOC over MNIST for a moderately sized spiking neural network.

Which is a lot.

And so this is kind of why we went to, like our lab, went to building our own computational neuroscience library.

And so that is called NGC Learn.

That is what I'm here to talk about today.

And basically, this is the little highlights reel and then we'll get into exactly how it's working.

But it is a public pip package.

You today, right now, could go and type in pip install NGC Learn.

And you will get a version of NGC Learn.

I will tell you right now, you're going to get 2.0, which is a perfectly functional version.

But it will not look exactly like what I'm talking about today.

NGC Learn at its core, though, is a state machine that sits next to JAX.

JAX, which is effectively the linear algebra library for NumPy that was the backbone of TensorFlow before it split.

And JAX allows us to do things that are known as just-in-time compilation, which allows us to take a bunch of Python code that we know has fixed sizes and fixed types and just produce effectively a C code snippet that we can run on GPUs, TPUs, CPUs, whatever.

And in doing this, our models in some preliminary results, you can get from something that takes about 8 hours written in TensorFlow to something that takes about 15 minutes on the same hardware written in NGC Learn.

And then the advantage of this is that it also allows for the creation of new methods, new models, new components, whatever, incredibly easily.

Because as long as you follow a very simple coding paradigm, everything will interface with it perfectly.

So let's get into what some of that looks like at a high level.

So if we're going to build a model in NGC Learn, we do have to slightly change how we are thinking about modeling a network.

Specifically, for us, it is just one giant state machine.

But if we just treat it as one giant state machine, it gets very hard to keep everything organized and not end up putting values where they're not supposed to be or just, you know, moving all the data around.

So we use components inside of our state machine, which here we're calling a context.

We use components to kind of hold parts of the state together and modify only a sub part of our state and then be able to communicate with other components, which just also encompass a different part of our state.

And this whole component model just allows for us to keep easy track.

And it's basically bookkeeping that allows the end user to say, I'm going to take these same values and do something new with them.

Similarly, inside of a component, we have a compartment model, and this is where data is stored and is moved around.

And this is what allows us to very easily move data from one component into another component without a lot of overhead.

So more specifically, looking at components here, here we have one for what is known as the leaky integrate and fire neuron.

Now, specifically, the math that happens inside of this isn't particularly relevant.

But know that this is one of the most common neurons used in computational neuroscience for modeling.

What it is, is it is effectively a it's effectively a capacitor where it slowly builds up current, passes a threshold, discharges, emits a spike.

Now, what this means is that there are some fixed parameters.

If we look here, the number of unit, the number of neurons in our like layer of leaky integrate and fire neurons is fixed.

We're not growing new neurons magically as this is going.

That voltage threshold that it's slowly building up to, that's also fixed.

We have some time constants.

We have a resistance value and we have some voltage rest and reset values.

These are all fixed parameters.

These don't change in time.

You set them up once at the beginning of your model and then you just call run.

Now, they can vary from component to component.

But as far as time is concerned, they are fixed.

Then we get to the compartments, which are the variable parts in time of our component.

So here we have current as an input into our component at a various time.

We will have a current.

Similarly, if we pause at a different time, we will have a voltage.

And at that same voltage, maybe we have an output spike.

And we also have the last time that we spiked.

So we keep a track of all of these values that change in time.

And this allows us to think about our components in two steps.

We have here's every we have here's all of the math and the logic and the fixed parameters.

And then here we have a couple of values that we need to make sure are updated at the end of every time we are called.

So that way we keep up to date with our state.

Finally, how do we connect these together?

So if we have component A and we have its parameters, which are our fixed values, and we have our compartments, which are our variable values.

And then we have component B, which has different parameters and different compartment values.

But we want to take the output of component A, and we want to make sure that it gets put as the input to component B.

Oh, sorry.

That just means that you're just going to take the value here, and it just gets passed along.

And it's done, and it traverses along what is known as a cable.

And so inside your model, you can take any compartment value, and you can wire it with a cable into any other compartment value.

And what that means is that when component B looks for the value inside this compartment, it will just know that it actually should be looking at the value inside this compartment.

And so this is how that we can keep things nice and fast, is there's no duplicates of values.

But effectively, it traverses along it.

Now, with component X, Y, and Z down here, we're doing something a little special.

We have a summation.

So now it's entirely reasonable to assume that the inputs into our component need, like, we could have a variable number of inputs.

One of the best examples of this is if you have a recurrent connection inside of your model that are both feeding into, say, the current value of your LIF neuron.

What that means is that you would have going down your model, you would have the current coming in, and then you have current from somewhere up in your model also looping around.

And basically there, we might want to add these together.

We might want to subtract them together.

We might want to have them multiplied and have some basic arithmetic operation done to them.

And so we allow you to do that along these cables.

You can basically bundle these cables together and then just say, hey, add all of these together.

And you can do that.

And this compartment here knows how to do all of that.

So when you go and try and see the value of this compartment, it will automatically collect these values, add them together, and use it.

And you, as the end user, don't have to make sure that that happens.

And if, for instance, we unplug component Y from the model, it just runs.

It all just works together.

Now, this shows us how to set up our model, but we're a state machine.

So we actually have to be able to transform through states.

So transitioning our model through time.

Here we have component A.

And here we have two different state transitions that we want to account for.

We have our reset transition, and we have our advanced transition.

Now, there's this little helpful key up here where anything that's a solid line means that that depends on those values.

And if it's a dashed line, that means it updates those values.

So we'll notice here inside of our reset function, it depends on this fixed parameter and this fixed parameter.

And then what it does is it just updates every compartment value inside the component.

Now, in the advanced state of our state transition, we'll notice that it takes this compartment value, this compartment value, this compartment value in, as well as these two fixed parameters.

And then it updates this compartment and this compartment over here.

And you can set these up in as complex of methods as you want.

Here, any amount of math can happen.

The only thing that we have to keep in mind is that these values are fixed and these values change in time.

That is the only thing that we need to keep in mind with this.

So if we want to do this across multiple components or what we call a process on our model, basically what would happen is we have some overarching process here.

We just have it called advance.

And what it's going to do is it's going to call advance here on component A.

It's going to compute all of its outputs.

Then since these are wired together, when we call advance over here on component B, it'll actually look at the output of component A and use it for component B.

And then it will compute its output and put it inside its output compartment.

And if we were to keep chaining and have a component C over here that was wired out, it would just keep going.

And so this allows you to chain as many of these components together inside like a forward pass as you want.

It's also important to note that these do not have to be the same type of component.

They do not have to be the same type of transition.

So here it's all named advanced.

But as we'll see later, it doesn't always have to be named.

It doesn't all have to be named advanced.

And so then, yeah, and it's always deterministic, the order that all of this data gets transmitted in.

It will always go in the order you define the components in.

So you don't have to worry about race conditions and anything like that.

So finally, in order to actually get speed up from this, because right now we've just written basic Python code, we actually have to go through what is known as a compiling step of our process.

And this makes use of Python's abstract syntax trees, which I am not going to get into the complications behind getting syntax trees to work.

But basically, we look at your code that you have written in the generic Python classes, and we tweak it just a little bit.

And we'll see how it tweaks it just a little bit to produce code that is fast and efficient for JAX's just-in-time compilation.

This is the true source of NGC Learn's speedup, is that it can take code that is written in a very easy-to-read, effectively format, and then convert it into what is needed to be efficient for JAX's just-in-time compilations format.

And so basically, we just take that whole process and we send it through our compiler.

Now, the compiler as an end user is not something that you need to ever actually worry about, but there are advantages.

But with V3, and this is what we're getting into here with V3, there are some things that you gain from, that you gain that allow you to look and really inspect at what is our model doing?

What is our process actually doing? Which is very helpful for things like debugging.

as a control bias means that we wear the body backwards. And the green areas are the axial

postural areas, the bony affordances, largely in the front, with the exception of the scapula.

And these are the areas, if you think about your body now, the most sensitive areas of the body,

but are these the areas where we experience the most problems? These are the areas where I think

that we experience the most repression and sometimes even shame. And when we see ostral collapse,

we see a collapse in these areas with a tremendous amount of stress and tension and compensation

required in their opposites. And those are the red areas. So when we have a head forward position,

when we have collapse in this anterior structure, the back of the neck is carrying this heavy,

imbalanced lobe, we get tension in the occiput, in the neck, and in the shoulders. When we

fail to attend to the proprioceptive prior in the armpit, we make the shoulder do all the work. And of

course, that's the third most common site of musculoskeletal complaints. It is not where we're

meant to control the body. We're meant to find coordinative awareness in these concavities.

And there are bony cerebral mechanisms of a low back. In relationship to the groin, the pubic bone in

the front, the pelvis very much is shaped like a wheelbarrow. And those two big bones in the back,

they're like the two big wheels or handles of a wheelbarrow. And we try our best to force that load.

And we believe that we can control that load with brute strength until the front wheel of the

wheelbarrow hits a pebble and the entire thing tips. So if we don't attend to the little front wheel to

the low front, then we're going to have dysfunction in the low back. The feet, what I'm going to say

about the feet is that that big toe is very clearly the power structure of the entire foot.

And what's the first thing we do when we put it in modern shoes? Hallux, valgus, we collapse, we crush

that structure. So that's my talk for today. And that's an effort to unpack belief updating and

exteroceptive bias in a somatic way with reference to Sherrington, with reference to anatomy, myofascial

anatomy. It's been a real pleasure. I want to ask you if you've made it this far in the talk,

All right, the next talk is by Sid. 10 minutes long.

Greetings, everyone. My name is Siddhant. And I'll walk you through our recent work on enhancing

Byzantine-tolerant formation control for maritime buoy swarms using, you guessed it right, active

inference. What are maritime buoy swarms? As you can see on my screen, maritime buoy swarms are

a swarm of buoys deployed in the ocean in order to achieve certain tasks. For example, if there is an

oil spill or like an incident in the ocean, these buoys help in monitoring that. They also help support

missions like surveillance, search and rescue, and other kinds of environmental monitoring in hostile

or contested waters. The threats that we focus on in this particular work rely on the configuration

that you see in front of you. All of these buoys can be imagined as decentralized robots floating in

the ocean with minimal motor control, and they can communicate among each other and a centralized hub.

But because of this, they are also prone to cyber physical attacks. So we will focus on three kinds of

attacks which can impact their operation and mission success. So for example, if the task is area coverage,

like taking down one or more of these buoys is equivalent to a Byzantine problem where we cannot

trust certain buoys anymore. And now we have to measure how to measure the mission success in this

particular scenario so that the rest of the buoys can reorganize around this. And in order to do that,

they need to detect the cyber physical attack at play. So the threats that we will focus on are GPS

spoofing, communication jamming, and actuator hijacking. Why these three? Because these three

push the resilience limits of existing control architectures which may not use artificial

intelligence or any active inference approach. Our goal in this work is to maintain formation integrity

and task coverage while detecting and mitigating malicious agents. I'll start with our baseline

approach and then explain how we integrated active inference, where it shines, where it still struggles,

what alternatives we considered, and how we plan to push the results further. The original methodology

without active inference uses a decentralized neighbor-to-neighbor approach in order to facilitate

communication. Each buoy assessed trust using handcrafted manual heuristics, rules that downweighted

agents exhibiting inconsistent trajectories, communication dropouts, or mission throughput anomalies. We combined that

multi-dimensional trust score with an attack-aware formation controller which essentially helped

re-weight the neighbor influence based on trust estimates while enforcing formation geometry and

coverage. This approach was practical and interpretable and it handled many different kinds of anomalies well,

as you can see in the graph in front of you. However, it lacked formal uncertainty quantification which is very important for this kind of a task.

It struggled with subtle or staged attacks that we inflicted upon the system and sometimes even over penalized honest agents during complicated maneuvers.

In our improvement methodology, like we see, we see that we introduced an active inference based trust assessor. The core idea here is to move from heuristic rules to a probabilistic generative model and variational Bayesian inference.

Each buoy in this new model maintains an internal state predicting neighbor trajectory's position, velocity, and acceleration, and even jerk to measure change in momentum while taking into account estimated formation forces.

When reality deviates from predictions, we compute a mahalanobis distance across those dimensions. High prediction errors suggest potential tampering. We then update our trust beliefs using Bayesian filtering, explicitly tracking both the mean trust score and its uncertainty. The formation controller uses those uncertainty-aware trust estimates to moderate neighbor influence.

Agent communication stays decentralized. The key change is that the trust updates now come from predictive modeling rather than fixed thresholds.

And this, as you can see, improves the detection accuracy a bit across all three metrics.

Where active inference is useful can be highlighted in the following ways.

Like, the graphs are pretty clear that active inference over a period of time can help in improving the results that we are seeking.

But to do that, it helps mostly in three areas.

First is early detection.

Predictive errors surface anomalies before they manifest as gross deviation.

We see this particularly with GPS spoofing position when they diverge sharply from expected trajectories.

Secondly, uncertainty quantification helps us in carrying variances along with trust means so that we can protect the honest agents when the model is unsure.

This reduces false positives during tight maneuvers.

Thirdly, it helps with attack specificity.

Different attack modes produce distinct error signatures which are important for us to quantify and measure.

GPS spoofing elevates the position residuals.

Communication jamming shows up in missing observations.

Actuator hijacking disrupts velocity and jerk.

We calibrate these responses accordingly.

In our V2 results, this translated into an 8.4% average improvement in detection accuracy,

a 1.6% boost in resilience, and 6.6% gain in task coverage with actuator hijacking coverage jumping nearly 20%.

And the results are pretty clear.

We do not claim that active inference is always the better approach.

We can see here that active inference does a lot better at task coverage and resilience score,

whereas it is nearly as performant in other metrics or sometimes less performant in average trust score over time.

Where active inference still falters is that the generative model still simplifies the hydrodynamics and control inputs.

When cooperative agents execute aggressive maneuvers, prediction errors spike, and the system can result in false alarms.

Moreover, communication jamming creates data gaps that challenge the inference updates.

Even with tempered thresholds, detection improvements for actuator hijacking are minuscule.

So what do we do?

We see that detection improvements are modest with only 3.5% because actuation dynamics are hard to model at the granularity we need.

So active inference is computationally heavier for these decentralized swarms with lesser processing budgets.

So we need reliable processing power in order to ensure real-time updates,

which may not always be a solution in a maritime environment.

The alternative approaches that we have considered was a reinforcement learning based trust estimation,

where agents learn policies from simulated attacks.

It offers adaptability but lacks the interpretability and uncertainty guarantees that we wanted.

Another was a consensus-based filtering with fault-tolerant observers, classic control theory techniques.

These have strong stability proofs but require centralized coordination or strict communication assumptions.

Another thing that we could look at is a hybrid approach which combines Kalman filters with heuristic gating,

which has the potential to show some promise, yet it also has the potential to lack explicit uncertainty handling.

What active inference gives us is a balance between theory, interpretability and decentralized operation.

The key finding for us is that active inference can outperform heuristic baselines across detection, resilience and coverage,

while providing principled uncertainty estimates.

GPS spoofing detection jumped over 15%.

Our communication jamming detection improved by 7%.

And actuator hijacking coverage improved almost 20%.

These are not basic numbers. These are quite significant.

So how do we go about it in a practical setting?

We confirm that these protective floors and attack-specific calibrations are essential.

Without them, active inference overreacts.

Finally, the predictive modeling gives the commanders richer diagnostic signals, not just what went wrong,

but how confident the system is in that assessment.

This is where active inference really shines.

We can improve upon a few things as next steps.

Our next steps focus on richer generative models incorporating better hydrodynamic parameters for this environment,

external disturbance introduction, and learned control inputs.

We plan to fuse communication reliability signals more tightly with behavioral predictions to handle jamming gaps.

We also want to explore hierarchical active inference, where local agents share compressed summaries of their beliefs to enhance collective awareness without breaking decentralization.

Finally, we plan to benchmark against adaptive consensus and reinforcement learning hybrids to ensure that we are harnessing the best aspects of each approach.

In summary, active inference has pushed our maritime swarm resilience forward in a simulation environment, giving us predictive confidence and measurable improvements against Byzantine threats.

The work is not done.

We need to do a lot more to move this from a Python-based simulation environment to the real world.

But the path is clearer.

We need to sharpen the models, strengthen the communication inference loop, and validate under more diverse operational profiles.

Thank you.

Please feel free to reach out, and I'm happy to dive deeper into any section.

And it has been an honor presenting here at the symposium, and I wish you luck.

Thank you.

Sciences and at the Okinawa Institute for Science and Technology. I study how people come to feel

together. Today, I want to talk about a problem that's obvious to anyone who has ever been in a

therapy room. Mental health interventions depend on attunement. When therapists and clients are not

aligned, therapy stalls or even fails. Yet we have almost no quantitative tools for monitoring attunement

in real time. Nikolas and I propose a way forward. We build on active inference models of social

interaction and link them to a geometric hyperscanning observable that is suitable for

clinical deployment. We will also sketch a prospective digital twin for psychotherapy that

uses real-time monitoring of neural geometry to mark and eventually predict when crucial behavioral

events, say ruptures, co-regulation, and reattunement occur. The common scientific approach to social

cognition is to treat one person as an observer, trying to infer the hidden mental state of another.

This third-person stance assumes social understanding happens through detached, one-way observation.

In contrast, second-person neuroscience emphasizes that meaningful interaction is embodied and

reciprocal. When two people engage, their brains form a coupled generative system.

Computationally, each partner maintains a generative model of their own, as well as of the other person's

states. Predictions are continuously updated and belief updating becomes mutual. This dissolves

the regress of I model you modeling me modeling you into a shared dynamic narrative.

And this framework, the dyad, not the isolated individual, is the basic unit of inference.

Affect fits naturally into this picture. Emotional valence can be formalized as inference over the

coherence of the self-model. Two quantities drive it. The magnitude of self-model prediction error,

so how surprised you are about yourself, and the self-relevance of that error, so how much the

situation touches your identity. We include an effective memory term to account for emotional

this is going to be a 20 minute video hello my name is sanjeev namjoshi and i'm a machine learning

engineer working at versus if you've ever tried to learn active inference you probably know what it

feels like to get lost in a maze of sub-disciplines neuroscience information theory bayesian statistics

physics and more every paper seems to assume you'll already know three other fields

with different mathematical notations equations seem to materialize out of nowhere with few good

resources explaining where they came from or how they're even useful in the first place

today i would like to talk to you about my progress toward providing greater accessibility

visibility and knowledge of active inference and the free energy principle

i'm excited to announce that my textbook fundamentals of active inference is complete

and will be released in march 2026 from the mit press my aim for this textbook is to provide a unified

systematic and rigorous foundation for students and researchers entering the field to reduce the

steep learning curve however an important motivating question is why should you learn active inference in

2025 to answer this question i think it is helpful to turn the clock back 2006 and draw a comparison

between the state of deep learning in its early days and active inference as we understand it today

prior to the mid-2000s neural networks were just one of many possible models rather than the dominant

choice in academia and industry this all changed in 2006 when hinton and his colleagues released the

deep belief network paper which is generally understood as the start of deep learning as we understand it today

after some various hardware innovations and the release of the well-known image net library

we started to see more coverage and success of ai in the news but in 2012 deep learning truly provided

its value with alex net and for the first time it achieved better than human performance on image

detection tasks

but this was just a start what followed was a proliferation of deep learning across industry and

research i've added some well-known milestones just to highlight the explosion of progress in deep

learning in the last decade but there is so much more that deserves mention and attention here

and since i presented about my textbook at the 2023 active inference symposium the state of ai has

dramatically changed once again today large language models dominate the ai landscape and have managed to

overshadow many other fields and alternative approaches new and more powerful language models are released

every quarter though the rate of advancement over previous models is beginning to slow so what about

the state of active inference as a field from my perspective active inference lies in the same position

as deep learning did in 2006 influential and on the brink of exploding in popularity and relevance

this paper here from which this figure comes from is from contributors at the active inference institute

and shows the current growth of publications in the active inference community

in the last few years active inference as a field has seen a number of important milestones

here i show just a few that broaden the scope and attention to the field

we have the first international workshop on active inference in 2020

we have the first applied active inference symposium and the foundation of the active inference institute

we have the release of the par pazulo and friston 2022 book as well as the pi mdp library

and from 2023 to present there are so many pieces and changes here some of which include new julia

packages rx infer and many many more papers in robotics and other industries as the international

workshop of active inference has begun to grow and grow and grow i see active inference as perfectly poised

for greater visibility and attention especially given the current interest in generative ai and deep

reinforcement learning working alongside the active inference institute and other organizations

my aim here is to provide some of the fundamental materials to capture the attention and notice of

machine learning researchers and students to bridge this gap to bring active inference into its renaissance

so why learn active inference in 2025 well active inference is highly interpretable the very nature of

bayesian networks means that you can follow the chain of reasoning through a particular problem to see why

a certain outcome was chosen over others for a particular model why a particular policy was chosen for example

active inference comes equipped with a principled modeling system a way to model any system of

interest using an elegant and all-encompassing compensing formalism based in information theory

active inference also has a more robust interpretation of agency than current llms as we talk about agency

in a lot of industry context today and includes an aspect of control of the external environment

active inference is fundamentally compatible with the notion of world models that capture the cause-effect

understanding of the external world and it's believed these world models are very important for

preventing hallucinations that we see in large language models active inference can also be used to

solve everyday practical problems in research and in industry and it may also expand the field of complex

systems modeling which is central to fields such as physics climate science economics political theory

psychology sociology and more active inference also provides a convenient way to univize unify many

disparate fields that are exploring the same ideas in different languages or scientific conventions

and although it's been yet to be fully demonstrated active inference does have the potential to be more

cost and sample efficient at scale and more research needs to go into this area which is why we need

a new generation of active inference researchers coming to contribute to the field

due to the current state of active inference and its potential as a premier ai research framework

my aim is to expand the reach of active inference beyond its current confines to this end i've aimed to

make my textbook as accessible for the widest audience possible with the least amount of required

prerequisites the major focus is writing this book for an engineering audience

both machine learning engineers and traditional engineering disciplines neuroscience is largely out

of scope for this book and there are many other resources available if you're interested in that

particular aspect of the free energy principle and active inference many of the recommended and even

optional prerequisites that you see here are typically known by undergraduate students in science and

engineering and certainly by graduate students in these fields nevertheless the book is also written

to be readable for those that desire to focus on everything the completionist that wants to know

all the math the concepts and the code it's also suited for people who are more interested in the theory

maybe just interested in the math and concepts to get a general sense of things so they can dive into

the literature further and my hope is that ultimately those who are intuitionists who want to skim over the

math and just get a sense of the ideas should still be able to read this book and ultimately i hope to

convince you by reading this textbook that active inference may be challenging but it is not impossible

statistical setting with an agent interacting with its environment we start as simple as possible with
